## File: docs/intent/database-adapters/intent-brief.md

<repository-file path="docs/intent/database-adapters/intent-brief.md">
---
title: "Database-backed analysis adapters"
slug: "database-adapters"
phase: intent
status: draft
last_updated: "2026-10-12"
---

# Database-backed analysis adapters

## My read

Analysts should explore real database data with the same responsive, trustworthy behavior they expect from data already loaded in the browser. A full client snapshot is useful when it fits the source and keeps filtering immediate. A smaller server result may be needed when a full snapshot is too large or too slow, but it must represent what the charts and inspection tools actually need. The current evidence does not set a row ceiling, latency promise, database choice, or query language.

The first useful step is a host-owned read of a registered source set into explorEDA’s existing `tables` input. That keeps current evaluation, chart filters, and record inspection in charge while a real database proves the boundary. PostgreSQL is the first driver candidate. A second driver should follow only if it tests whether a shared adapter boundary is useful. A complete snapshot may cost more transfer and browser memory than a narrow server query; measure that cost before choosing a broader query path.

The product is hosted as a dedicated application instance for each paying customer. Setup and routine operation should need little manual work. This defines the operating unit, but leaves the hosting platform, isolation details, budget, and customer database location open. This initiative supplies driver and connection constraints to the hosting work.

## What matters most

- Preserve correct field values, row identity, filter scope, inspection, and contributor meaning.
- Keep local filtering responsive when a complete snapshot fits.
- Make incomplete or unsupported results visible; never label partial data complete.
- Make per-customer setup and operation repeatable with low manual work.

## Boundaries

### Must be true

- Database access uses host-owned source definitions and permissions.
- A snapshot is complete, type-safe for its declared uses, and coherent across its sources, or it fails with a clear reason.
- Any later server query proves its own null, identity, join, aggregation, and coverage rules.
- Each substantial step leaves a useful, testable state.

### Must be avoided

- Do not claim SQL and JavaScript analysis parity from similar operation names.
- Do not silently lose precision, change field types, rebound selections, or publish a mixed snapshot.
- Do not introduce a universal SQL compiler, federation layer, or dual local/server fallback without a demonstrated need.

## What seems settled

PostgreSQL is the first driver proof. A second driver follows only when it can test a useful shared boundary. Keep local analysis in the first snapshot path. Require explicit evidence for type admission, null and identity behavior, joins, aggregation, completion, and cleanup.

## Current reality that matters

`ExplorEdaProject` accepts source row arrays through `tables` and evaluates queries locally, including source, lookup, expand, calculate, filter, and aggregate stages. The repository has no remote query execution prop. This is a useful first seam, not evidence that full snapshots meet a customer workload.

## Possibilities, not decisions

Binary transport, progressive row loading, SQL pushdown, SQLite or another second driver, DuckDB, and the hosting platform remain candidates. The notebook’s 10,000–100,000 rows describe an example workload, not a product limit.

## Next step after confirmation

Shape a bounded source-snapshot proof with PostgreSQL first. State the conditions that would justify a second driver and a narrower server query.

</repository-file>

## File: docs/intent/database-adapters/shape-brief.md

<repository-file path="docs/intent/database-adapters/shape-brief.md">
---
title: "Database-backed analysis adapters — shape brief"
slug: "database-adapters"
phase: shape
status: draft
last_updated: "2026-10-12"
---

# Database-backed analysis adapters — what we are adding

**Outcome:** Analysts can load registered database data into explorEDA and explore it with correct, responsive local behavior.

**Primary flow:** A customer’s host authenticates a read → loads one bounded, complete source snapshot → passes typed rows to `ExplorEdaProject.tables` → the existing evaluator and charts run locally.

## Feature scope

```text
Database-backed analysis
├── PLANNED ADDITIONS
│   ├── Host-owned source reader
│   │   ├── Reads only registered sources with restricted credentials
│   │   ├── Returns one complete, coherent snapshot or a typed failure
│   │   └── Enforces row, cell, byte, and time bounds before publication
│   ├── Declared source types and row identity
│   │   ├── Preserves supported values and rejects lossy conversions
│   │   └── Distinguishes stable keys from snapshot-only row identity
│   └── PostgreSQL proof
│       └── Keeps driver code and credentials on the host server
├── EVALUATE BEFORE COMMITTING
│   ├── A second driver, only if it tests a useful shared adapter boundary
│   └── One narrow SQL row or aggregate request, only after snapshot evidence
└── LATER POSSIBILITIES
    ├── Progressive or partial coverage with explicit consumer behavior
    ├── Cross-database federation and arbitrary server formulas
    └── DuckDB default, separate query service, or universal adapter platform
```

## Behavior

| Situation | Expected result |
| --- | --- |
| The reader obtains every approved row and field in one coherent read | Publish the snapshot with exact coverage and snapshot identity. |
| A source, field, type, limit, authorization, or read step is unsupported | Return a clear failure and publish no partial result as complete. |
| A stable key is missing | Use occurrence identity for this snapshot; refresh starts a new identity domain. |
| A planned server operation lacks proven semantics | Return `UNSUPPORTED`; do not silently use a partial server result with local evaluation. |

## Decisions and boundaries

**Appetite:** One host-owned reader path and one PostgreSQL proof. Keep current client evaluation and imported/demo data behavior. Do not create a package abstraction until a second real driver shows a stable shared boundary.

**Key decision:** Start with complete source snapshots because the current component already consumes source arrays and exposes evaluated stages and records. This preserves inspection and chart behavior while the data boundary is tested. A server row or aggregate path can reduce transfer, but needs separate proofs for coverage, filter context, identity, joins, and contributors.

**Boundary:** The browser sends registered source identifiers and bound values, never credentials, arbitrary hostnames, table names, or SQL. The host owns authentication, source registration, permissions, connection lifecycle, and per-customer settings. Preserve current local mode. Hosting platform choice stays with the hosting initiative.

**First proof:** **Try:** Load a small typed fixture through a deterministic fake and a disposable PostgreSQL instance; pass the complete rows through `tables`. **Observe:** Values and field types match, nulls stay distinct, stable and snapshot-only identity are labeled correctly, filters and inspection agree with the local fixture, all sources share one snapshot, limits reject before uncontrolled buffering, and query cleanup is observed. **Decide:** Continue only if parity and coverage hold and the workload meets an agreed response and memory budget. If full transfer fails, test one named projected-row or aggregate demand; if a shared abstraction has no demonstrated value, retain the fixed host reader.

**Open choices:** Which declared source types and precision ranges matter to the first supported customer data? What workload and response budget should the first integration satisfy?

See [intent brief](intent-brief.md) and [recommendation review](evidence/recommendation-review.md).

</repository-file>

## File: docs/intent/columnar-data-transport/intent-brief.md

<repository-file path="docs/intent/columnar-data-transport/intent-brief.md">
---
title: "Columnar data transport"
slug: "columnar-data-transport"
phase: shape
status: draft
last_updated: "2026-10-12"
---

# Columnar data transport

## My read

You want database-backed exploration to stay responsive while preserving the meaning of each record and view. A complete client snapshot is useful when its transfer and full resident cost fit. It is not required for every query or source. The first target is a bounded complete snapshot for representative work around 10,000–100,000 rows, with width and key size varied. This range guides the proof; it is not a hard product limit.

The transport choice is still open. Use a bounded complete source snapshot as the semantic baseline, then compare Arrow IPC with lossless, schema-described row JSON. Both candidates must use the same snapshot, HTTP compression, current row consumers, and completion rules. Count server encoding, decoder startup, row construction, provider setup, filtering, chart work, and memory during replacement. Adopt Arrow only if it preserves supported behavior and improves the complete path by a declared measure. Do not treat a smaller body as a user benefit by itself.

## What matters most

- Filtering and inspection must describe the same records and values as the source.
- Complete results and limited coverage must be distinguishable.
- The client must remain responsive within measured memory and copy budgets.
- Data, caches, and temporary results belong to one dedicated customer instance.

## The experience or behavior you appear to want

The user chooses a database-backed view and receives enough complete rows and fields to explore it accurately. Local filtering stays available where the snapshot contains the required population and fields. If the result is too large or unsupported, the system asks for a narrower complete projection, returns an exact aggregate with clear inspection limits, or reports the limit. It never presents silent truncation as complete data.

## Boundaries

### Must be true

- Results identify their source snapshot, row grain and key/order domain, schema, population scope, field and row coverage, counts, and successful completion.
- Unsupported or lossy value conversions fail with a clear capability result.
- The first application proof publishes one completed source snapshot into the existing row consumer boundary; any narrower projection states its complete row and field coverage.
- Encoding comparisons include the existing row-based provider, Crossfilter, chart, and trace costs.

### Must be avoided

- Joining fields by matching row counts alone.
- Calling a transport end-to-end zero-copy without a measured allocation ledger.
- Shipping both encodings as a runtime fallback or default dual-format system before the benchmark selects one.
- Sharing customer data or result caches across dedicated customer instances.

## What seems settled

- The binary candidate is Arrow IPC stream with no internal compression and negotiated HTTP gzip.
- Lossless row JSON is the simpler benchmark baseline.
- Parquet delivery, a custom codec, a resident column store, and a general progressive cache are outside the first proof.
- Snapshot identity, type exactness, coverage, and capacity are shared contracts with query, adapter, and hosting work.

## Next step after confirmation

Shape the bounded proof and its adoption gate. Keep platform selection and implementation sequencing with their owning work.

</repository-file>

## File: docs/intent/columnar-data-transport/shape-brief.md

<repository-file path="docs/intent/columnar-data-transport/shape-brief.md">
---
title: "Columnar data transport — shape brief"
slug: "columnar-data-transport"
phase: shape
status: draft
last_updated: "2026-10-12"
---

# Columnar data transport — what we are adding

**Outcome:** Database-backed views can use a bounded, complete result while preserving current row behavior and exact filtering.

**Primary flow:** Request one bounded complete source snapshot → validate identity, types, coverage, and completion → materialize rows → publish once → filter and inspect locally.

## Feature scope

```text
Bounded snapshot transport
└── PLANNED ADDITIONS
    ├── Complete source snapshot baseline
    │   ├── Keep current local evaluation, filtering, charts and inspection
    │   └── Allow a narrower projection only with complete declared row/field coverage
    ├── Complete result contract
    │   ├── Source snapshot, row grain, typed keys and order
    │   ├── Population scope, field/row coverage and exactness
    │   └── Schema, count meaning, completion and cancellation state
    ├── One selected response encoding
    │   ├── Compare Arrow IPC stream with lossless row JSON
    │   └── Apply the same HTTP compression and current row consumers
    ├── Atomic browser handoff
    │   ├── Decode and validate before provider publication
    │   └── Keep existing row-based filtering, charts and inspection
    └── Capacity and customer boundary
        ├── Count decoded, retained and peak client data, including copies
        └── Keep snapshots, caches and temporary data inside one customer instance
```

## Behavior

| Situation | Expected result |
| --- | --- |
| A bounded response matches its declared source, schema, row domain and expected counts | Publish it once as complete after successful end validation. |
| A batch is missing, duplicated, stale, reordered, truncated, or from a changed snapshot | Keep the prior coherent view and reject the incoming result. |
| The result omits rows or fields needed by a local filter or trace | Do not claim full local coverage; request a complete required projection or report the capability limit. |
| The source value cannot be represented by current consumers without loss | Return an explicit unsupported result before applying it. |
| The result exceeds a measured transfer or resident budget | Prefer a narrower complete projection or exact aggregate with truthful trace support; never silently return the first rows. |

## Decisions and boundaries

**Appetite:** One bounded complete-source proof at the current row boundary, with 10k and 100k fixtures. A narrower complete projection can be compared as an explicit variant. These row counts are starting fixtures, not a ceiling.

**Key decision:** Compare Arrow IPC against lossless row JSON under equal source, HTTP gzip, schema, commit timing, and row consumers. Benchmark both; then choose one production encoding. This tests whether column transport improves the whole view instead of only its wire size.

**Boundary:** First prove a complete source snapshot or complete projection. Do not build a general cache, streaming query engine, second runtime format, Parquet range reader, custom codec, or new resident column store. All memory budgets are provisional until the target browser, schema widths, keys, and provider costs are measured. Each paying customer owns its data and cache.

**First proof:** **Try:** Establish a complete 10k and 100k source snapshot through the existing project/evaluator path. Compare row JSON and Arrow at the current row boundary with the same real keys, field settings, charts, filters, and replacement lifecycle. **Observe:** Exact value and scope parity; request-to-filter-ready p95; first exact filter; encoded and decoded bytes; server CPU; decoder and row-bridge cost; retained and peak attributable memory with unique allocations and old/new overlap. **Decide:** Arrow is eligible only after correctness and capacity pass, plus either ≥20% and ≥100 ms p95 improvement or ≥25% lower peak attributable client data with ≤5% latency regression. Apply small-output and interaction guards. Otherwise choose the simpler format that passes, or stop transport work if current consumers dominate both.

**Cross-track contract:** Query work owns demand and population meaning. Adapter work owns source consistency, physical types, and fetch/cancel behavior. Hosting owns platform limits and automated lifecycle. All three must preserve authoritative snapshot identity, row grain/key/order, schema, scope, coverage, count meaning, completion, and instance ownership. See [intent](intent-brief.md) and [recommendation review](evidence/recommendation-review.md).

</repository-file>

## File: docs/intent/database-query-contract/intent-brief.md

<repository-file path="docs/intent/database-query-contract/intent-brief.md">
---
title: "Responsive database-backed exploration"
slug: "database-query-contract"
phase: intent
status: current
last_updated: "2026-10-12"
---

# Responsive database-backed exploration

## My read

Analysts should explore data from real databases with the responsive linked views they have today. Each paying customer has a dedicated application instance. That instance owns the source registry and resolves which database sources the user may read. The client should keep a complete snapshot of authorized source tables when their size and fields make that practical. A full snapshot is a preference, not a rule for every source. The 10,000 to 100,000 row range is a working example; row width and device capacity remain to be measured.

The central user task is to select a segment, compare linked charts, inspect matching records and analysis stages, and clear the selection. These actions must preserve row meaning, filter scope, and honest counts. The first integration should return complete authorized source snapshots into `ExplorEdaProject.tables`, then let the existing evaluator build stages and contributors locally. If data is partial or unavailable, the product must show that state and request more data before claiming an exhaustive result.

## What matters most

- Keep linked filtering responsive when the required data is complete in the client.
- Preserve exact row membership, field meaning, and source identity across views.
- Measure a named workload before choosing a more complex read strategy.

## The experience or behavior you appear to want

Open a database-backed analysis, view linked charts and records, select a category or brush a plot, then clear the selection. The charts and record list describe the same population. When the needed projection is cached completely, selection and clearing do not wait for another database read. When required fields or rows are missing, keep the previous result tied to its actual scope while new data loads.

## Boundaries

### Must be true

- The authored analysis keeps its row meaning. View demands and temporary filters do not rewrite it.
- Customer and source identity come from the authenticated server-side instance configuration, not a browser-supplied customer selector.
- A result states which rows, fields, source snapshot, and filter scope it covers.
- Unsupported or incomplete reads cannot become exact empty results.
- The library stays in React and TypeScript. Database credentials stay on the server.

### Must be avoided

- Silent truncation, missing fields treated as null, mixed snapshots, unstable page identity, or incomplete data labeled complete.
- A general query optimizer, arbitrary SQL interface, or progressive region cache before a named workload needs it.

## What seems settled

The durable goal is correct, responsive exploration of real database data. The complete snapshot remains desirable when feasible. Query construction, transport encoding, and database adapters are separate concerns that need a shared result identity and coverage contract.

## Possibilities, not decisions

Server aggregates, field expansion, compressed column transport, and snapshot reuse are candidates. Notebook sketches and the 10,000–100,000 row range are examples, not measured limits. The fourth hosting track evaluates one dedicated instance per paying customer; this track does not choose tenancy or hosting.

## Current reality that matters

The current `ExplorEdaProject` already accepts source tables and runs the existing evaluator locally or in its optional worker. It exposes evaluated stages and contributors to inspection. Crossfilter gives each chart an owner scope, and missing fields can currently resolve to all-undefined columns. A complete source loader reuses these seams; an output-only projection would require proof that it includes dependencies for every admitted operation and inspection path.

## Next step after confirmation

This intent and initial shaping work is authorized and complete. The proposed next product proof, after implementation is authorized, uses a host-owned source reader that resolves one customer's registered sources and returns complete immutable source arrays to the existing tables input. A deterministic fake tests the evaluator and linked views. Consider source-column projections only when their dependency closure preserves every admitted analysis and inspection behavior.

</repository-file>

## File: docs/intent/date-time-handling/intent-brief.md

<repository-file path="docs/intent/date-time-handling/intent-brief.md">
---
title: "Rework how dates and times work across the app"
slug: "date-time-handling"
phase: intent
status: draft
last_updated: "2026-10-10"
---

# Rework how dates and times work across the app

## My read

Byron wants one coherent pass over how explorEDA treats dates and times, rather than patching each chart. He has several ideas that are not yet written down. This seed holds the first concrete piece split out of other work, so it is not lost while the ideas are collected.

## What matters most

- Treat dates as one concern across import, fields, filters, and charts.
- Capture Byron's ideas before choosing a scope.

## The experience or behavior you appear to want

Not yet stated. Byron's ideas will define it.

## Boundaries

### Must be true

- Keep existing date behavior working until a change replaces it on purpose.

### Must be avoided

- Building piecemeal date controls chart by chart before the rework has a direction.

## What seems settled

- Date-axis bounds belong here, not in [axis-domain controls](../axis-domain-controls/intent-brief.md). That includes setting a date range on a time-series axis, typing exact dates, and dragging or resetting it.

## Possibilities, not decisions

- A date axis may reuse the numeric axis's drag and reset, with date entry in place of number fields.

## Current reality that matters

- Numeric axis bounds (`xAxis.limits`, `yAxis.limits`) and axis drag work only on numeric axes. A time-series X axis is excluded (`numericAxes` in `components/charts/Axis/axisBounds.ts`).
- The time-series date axis takes its range from the rows other charts leave in, so it moves on every brush. That breaks the stable-axes rule in `docs/ui-defaults.md`. A fix has to keep the "filter the dates in another view" route that the 2,000-period cap relies on.
- A field is typed as a date only when its values have a date shape (#231).
- [Calendar and period analysis](../calendar-period-analysis/intent-brief.md) already covers stepping through periods and rolling measures. The two should be reconciled once this rework has a direction.

## What still needs your input

What are your ideas for dates? A rough list is enough to start.

## Next step after confirmation

Recover intent from Byron's ideas, then reconcile the scope with calendar and period analysis.

</repository-file>
